Understanding this reduction in relation to the prior works on noise-resilience of quantum simulation
[
91
,
92
,
93
,
94
,
82
,
95
,
96
,
97
]
is left to future work.

Figure 8:

Circuit simulations with depolarizing noise applied to the 2-qubit gates for
n=5
qubits
and
\beta=(2J)^{-1}
.
The distance
\|\tilde{\rho}^{\rm circ}_{\infty}-\sigma_{\beta}\|_{1}
between the target Gibbs state and the output of the noisy circuits (circles) is reported as a function of the noise strength
\lambda_{\rm g}
in Eq. (
38
) for different values of the evolution step
\delta t
(colors in legend).
Further details regarding the parameters used for the circuit construction are provided in the main text.
These distances account for both algorithmic and noise errors in the preparation of the Gibbs state.
In addition, we report the bounds
\tilde{B}_{\infty}
on the noise errors from Eq. (
35
), shifted by the algorithmic error
d_{0}
, which is evaluated for
\lambda_{g}=0
.
We report these shifted bounds both for the actual number of gates
N_{\rm g}
used in the compiled circuits (dashed lines) and for an
effective
number of gates
N_{\rm eff}
(solid lines), which is obtained by fitting the bound
\tilde{B}_{\infty}+d_{0}
to the data. Both
N_{g}
and the fitted values
N_{\rm eff}
are provided in the main text for all the
\delta t
studied.

8
Conclusion

Our contribution bridges the gap between the predominantly theory-driven literature on dissipative quantum Gibbs state preparation algorithms, and the engineering of concrete Lindbladians and protocols that exhibit good convergence in practice, with circuits implementable on foreseeable quantum hardware.
To this end, we combine several recent developments in the literature
[
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
38
,
60
]
and propose a variant of this class of algorithms with reduced implementation cost (Secs.
3
and
4
).
Through a numerical analysis (Sec.
5
), we establish the crucial influence of the dynamical properties of the underlying system Hamiltonian and the Lindblad operators on mixing time and convergence accuracy.
In line with our theoretical analysis, we observe a weak, almost linear, polynomial system-size scaling of the mixing time for systems obeying the ETH.
In contrast, the Lindblad dynamics exhibits vastly different convergence characteristics in the non-chaotic limits of our model.

In realistic scenarios, the overall Gibbs state preparation error is controlled by an interplay of algorithmic and hardware-induced errors—for example smaller evolution steps in Eq. (
19
) reduce the Trotterization error but, at the same time, increase the gate count of the full circuit.
To obtain a good understanding of those error sources,
we investigate their impact both on a theoretical level and based on circuit simulations (Secs.
4
,
6
and
7
).
We numerically demonstrate that our theoretical error bounds provide a good description of the actual errors in noiseless circuit simulations.
Further, we show that the Lindblad dynamics can exhibit an inherent resilience against incoherent stochastic noise, due to the presence of a nontrivial steady state of the noisy dissipative dynamics.
Unlike in the unitary case, noise-induced errors at earlier times can be damped significantly through the dissipative character of the dynamics, which is a promising observation for the successful demonstration of this class of algorithms on near-term hardware.
We support our theoretical noise analysis with circuit simulations considering a local depolarizing noise model.

The successful demonstration of dissipative quantum Gibbs state preparation algorithms on foreseeable hardware requires a deep understanding of many contributing factors—dynamical and algorithmic design properties (Hamiltonian, Lindblad operators, initial state, algorithmic parameters), and of the expected hardware errors.
This work lays an important foundation to achieve this goal. Future work needs to address the design of suitable initial states implementable with shallow circuits, and a detailed account of the trade-offs between algorithmic and noise-induced errors for realistic hardware-specific noise models.
To further mitigate the influence of noise, one may design the Lindbladian to take into account and counteract pre-characterized stochastic noise channels.
Furthermore, the influence of coherent errors, and potential routes to convert those into stochastic noise similar to randomized compiling
[
87
,
98
]
, need to be explored.
Finally, to assess the performance of the algorithm, efficient ways to certify the prepared Gibbs state, e.g. based on recent Hamiltonian learning results
[
99
,
45
]
, are required.

Acknowledgements.

We thank Daniel Stilck França, Tomoya Hayata, and Maria Tudorovskaya for insightful discussions. We thank Marcello Benedetti and Henrik Dreyer for their feedback on this manuscript.

Appendix A
Protocol details and relation to the CKG algorithm

After briefly reviewing the quantum Gibbs sampling algorithm proposed in
[
32
,
33
]
, we clarify its relation to our protocol.

A.1
CKG Quantum Gibbs sampling algorithm

The CKG algorithm
[
33
]
uses the Lindbladian

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CKG}}[\rho]&=-\mathrm{i}[G,\rho]+\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\,\gamma(\omega)\Big(L^{a}(\omega)\rho L^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{L^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}L^{a}(\omega),\rho\}\Big)\\
&=-\mathrm{i}[G,\rho]+\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\alpha_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\Big(A^{a}_{\nu_{1}}\rho(A^{a}_{\nu_{2}})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{(A^{a}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{a}_{\nu_{2}},\rho\}\Big),\\
L^{a}(\omega)&=\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}\hat{g}_{\rm CKG}(\nu-\omega)A_{\nu},\end{split}

(39)

where
\alpha_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\coloneq\int\mathrm{d}\omega\gamma(\omega)\hat{g}_{\rm CKG}(\nu_{1}-\omega)\hat{g}_{\rm CKG}(\nu_{2}-\omega)
and
B_{H}
is the set of Bohr frequencies of the Hamiltonian
H
of the target Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}
.
With the choice of transition weight
\gamma(\omega)=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(\omega+\omega_{\gamma})^{2}}{2\Delta_{\gamma}^{2}}}
and frequency-domain filter function
\hat{g}_{\rm CKG}(\omega)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2})^{1/4}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{4\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}}}
for parametrization satisfying
\beta=\frac{2\omega_{\gamma}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}+\Delta_{\gamma}^{2}}
, one can readily confirm the identity,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/2}\alpha_{-\nu_{1},-\nu_{2}}.

(40)

This identity ensures that the transition term
\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\alpha_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}A^{a}_{\nu_{1}}[\cdot](A^{a}_{\nu_{2}})^{\dagger}
obeys the
\sigma_{\beta}
-DB condition (
5
), while the decay term
-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\{L^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}L^{a}(\omega),\cdot\}
does not.
To enforce the
\sigma_{\beta}
-DB condition for the full Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CKG}}[\cdot]
, the authors in
[
33
]
designed the coherent term as

\displaystyle G=G_{\rm CKG}\coloneq\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\alpha_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\tanh\Big(\frac{\beta(\nu_1-\nu_2)}{4}\Big)(A^{a}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{a}_{\nu_{2}},

(41)

which, combined with the decay term, obeys the
\sigma_{\beta}
-DB condition, and thus, so does the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CKG}}
,

\displaystyle\langle X,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CKG}}^{\dagger}[Y]\rangle_{\sigma_{\beta}}=\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CKG}}^{\dagger}[X],Y\rangle_{\sigma_{\beta}}

(42)

for any bounded operators
X,Y
.

A.2
Relation to the present protocol

In the present work, we choose a
\delta
-function for the transition weight

\displaystyle\gamma(\omega)=\delta(\omega+\omega_{\gamma})\quad\text{with}\quad\omega_{\gamma}=\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}/2.

(43)

With such a choice, the coefficients
\alpha_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
factorize as

\displaystyle\alpha_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}=\eta_{\nu_{1}}\eta_{\nu_{2}}\quad\text{with}\quad\eta_{\nu}\coloneq\hat{g}_{\rm CKG}(\nu+\omega_{\gamma})=\frac{1}{(2\pi\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2})^{1/4}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(\nu+\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}/2)^{2}}{4\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}}},

(44)

and satisfy the identity (
40
).
Furthermore, the Lindbladian (
39
) is reduced to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CKG}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}[G,\rho]+\gamma\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\Big(L^{a}\rho L^{a{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\{L^{a{\dagger}}L^{a},\rho\}\Big),

(45)

\displaystyle L^{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,g(t)A^{a}(t)=\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}\eta_{\nu}A^{a}_{\nu},

(46)

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=G_{\rm CKG}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B_{H}}\eta_{\nu_{1}}\eta_{\nu_{2}}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}{4}\right)(A^{a}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{a}_{\nu_{2}},

(47)

with
A^{a}(t)\coloneq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Ht}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Ht}
,
\eta_{\nu}\coloneq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu t}g(t)
,
A^{a}_{\nu}\coloneq\sum_{E_{i}-E_{j}=\nu}\Pi_{i}A^{a}\Pi_{j}
and
g(t)
as defined in Eq. (
9
).
Equation (
45
) is identical to our Lindbladian (
3
) except for the coherent term involving
G_{\rm CKG}
and the fact that transition rates
\gamma_{a}
in Eq. (
3
) formally depend on
a
because subsequently we write
\gamma_{a}=\gamma p_{a}
and sample individual Lindblad operators according to the discrete distribution
p_{a}
.

Next, we show that the coherent part (
47
) vanishes under the ETH average, i.e.
\mathbb{E}_{R}[G_{\rm CKG}]=0
.
To this end, we assume that the jump operators
A^{a}
satisfy the ETH (
11
),

\displaystyle\bra{E_i}A^{a}\ket{E_j}=\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{i})\delta_{ij}+\frac{f^{a}(E_{ij},\nu_{ij})}{\sqrt{D(E_{ij})}}R^{a}_{ij},

(48)

where the random variables
R^{a}_{ij}
are independent and satisfy
\mathbb{E}_{R}[R^{a}_{ij}]=0
together with
\mathbb{E}_{R}[|R^{a}_{ij}|^{2}]=1
, and
D(E)
is the density of states. Hence, the ETH average of a product of jump operators is given by

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{R}[(A^{a}_{\nu_{2}})^{\dagger}A_{\nu_{1}}^{a}]&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E_{k}-E_{i}=\nu_{2}\\
E_{k}-E_{j}=\nu_{1}\end{subarray}}\delta_{ki}\delta_{kj}\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{i})\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{k})\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}+\frac{f^{a}_{ki}f^{a}_{kj}}{\sqrt{D(E_{ki})D(E_{kj})}}\mathbb{E}_{R}[R^{a\ast}_{ki}R^{a}_{kj}]\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E_{k}-E_{i}=\nu_{2}\\
E_{k}-E_{j}=\nu_{1}\end{subarray}}\ket{E_i}\bra{E_i}\Big(\delta_{\nu_{1},0}\delta_{\nu_{2},0}(\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{i}))^{2}+\delta_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{|f^{a}_{ki}|^{2}}{D(E_{ki})}\Big).\end{split}

(49)

In the first line, we use the fact that the cross terms involving
\mathcal{A}^{a}
and
R^{a}
are first-order in the random matrix elements
R^{a}_{ij}
and, hence, vanish in the ETH average.
For the second line note that the
\delta_{ki}\delta_{kj}
in the first term enforces
E_{k}=E_{i}=E_{j}
, such that
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}=0
.
Moreover,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{R}}[R^{a\ast}_{ki}R^{a}_{kj}]=\delta_{ij}
, which enforces
E_{i}=E_{j}
and, thus,
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}
.
Importantly, both of these contributions are zero whenever
\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}
.
From this, and the fact that
\tanh(0)=0
, it directly follows that the coherent term (
47
) vanishes under the ETH:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{R}[G]\propto\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}\tanh\left(\frac{\beta\nu}{4}\right)\sum_{\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}=\nu}\eta_{\nu_{1}}\eta_{\nu_{2}}\delta_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}=0.\end{split}

(50)

The above argument confirms that the average dissipative Lindbladian
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{D}
, with

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\rho]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}\gamma_{a}\Big(L^{a}\rho L^{a{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\{L^{a{\dagger}}L^{a},\rho\}\Big).

(51)

[cf. Eq. (
3
)], is
\sigma_{\beta}
-DB without introducing the coherent term
-\mathrm{i}[G_{\rm CKG},\cdot]
, as we saw this in Sec.
3.2
.
Since the Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}
commutes with
H
, a coherent term
-\mathrm{i}[G,\cdot]
generated by the system Hamiltonian
G=H
does not change the steady state, and
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}=-\mathrm{i}[H,\cdot]+\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{D}
remains
\sigma_{\beta}
-DB under the ETH average under a slightly generalized definition of detailed balance.
To see this, note that, in general,
(\mathcal{L}^{\dagger})^{\dagger_{\mathrm{KMS}}}=\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/2}\mathcal{L}[\sigma_{\beta}^{1/2}\,\cdot\,\sigma_{\beta}^{1/2}]\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/2}
is the adjoint of
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}
(the Hilbert-Schmidt adjoint of
\mathcal{L}
) with respect to the KMS inner product
\expectationvalue{\cdot, \cdot}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
.
In our case both generators are not equal, as required by our KMS detailed balance condition (
5
).
Their difference (under the ETH average) is given by
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}-(\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger})^{\dagger_{\mathrm{KMS}}}=2\mathrm{i}[H,\cdot]
, as can be readily confirmed. This is a special case of the more general version of quantum detailed balance introduced in
[
54
]
(Def. 27).
In fact, it is easy to see [similar as in Eq. (
6
)], that under this condition

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}[\sigma_{\beta}],X\rangle_{\rm HS}&=\expectationvalue*{I, \bE_R\calL^\dag[X]}_{\sigma_{\beta}}=\expectationvalue*{(\bE_R\calL^\dag)^{\dag_\mathrm{KMS}} [I], X}_{\sigma_{\beta}}=\expectationvalue*{\bE_R\calL^\dag[I] - 2\im[H, I], X}_{\sigma_{\beta}}=0\end{split}

(52)

for all
X
, which confirms that
\sigma_{\beta}
is the steady state of
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
.

Thus, by replacing
G_{\mathrm{CKG}}
in Eq. (
45
) with the system Hamiltonian
H
, we end up with our Lindbladian (
3
).
For our protocol, we set
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}=\sqrt{2}/\beta
(see App.
C.1
where we elaborate on this choice of parameter), which results in the filter function [cf. Eq. (
9
)]

\displaystyle g(t)=\frac{1}{\pi^{3/4}\beta^{1/2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t/\beta}\mathrm{e}^{-2t^{2}/\beta^{2}}.

(53)

With this, our Lindblad operators take the form [cf. Eq. (
8
)]

\displaystyle L^{a}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,g(t)A^{a}(t)=\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}\right)^{1/4}\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(\beta\nu+1)^{2}}{8}}A^{a}_{\nu}=\sum\eta_{\nu}A^{a}_{\nu},

(54)

with the frequency-domain filter function

\displaystyle\eta_{\nu}=\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}\right)^{1/4}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(\beta\nu+1)^{2}}{8}}.

(55)

Appendix B
Details of single-ancilla Gibbs state preparation protocol

We provide details on the implementation of the steps of Lindblad evolution that are core to our Gibbs-state preparation protocol by closely following
[
34
]
, where the authors employed a single-ancilla protocol to prepare ground states.
A thorough error analysis of each approximation necessary for a quantum circuit implementation is provided (Sec.
B.1
to Sec.
B.3
).
A summary of these contributions is reported in Sec.
B.4
together with the overall circuit complexity,
which we state here as a main result:

Theorem 1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the Lindbladian given by Eq. (
56
) with the Lindblad operators (
78
). For simulation time
t
and inverse temperature
\beta
, there exists a quantum algorithm that simulates a Lindblad dynamics
\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{L}t}[\cdot]
with an error
\epsilon
in trace distance, whose cost in terms of the Hamiltonian simulation time is given by
\Theta\big(\frac{\beta t^{2}}{\epsilon}\sqrt{\mathrm{log}\frac{\beta t}{\epsilon}}\big)
.

The theorem is proven in the remainder of this App.
B
. See Eq. (
102
) for the final result.
A decomposition of the algorithmic error into individual contributions is given in Prop.
1
at the end of this appendix.
In the following, we aim at implementing the evolution
\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}}
for a Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
over a simulation time
t=M\delta t
.
The Lindbladian (
3
) with
G=H
can be recast as

\mathcal{L}[\rho]=\sum_{a\in\bm{A}}p_{a}\left(-\mathrm{i}[H,\rho]+\gamma\Big(L^{a}\rho L^{a{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\{L^{a{\dagger}}L^{a},\rho\}\Big)\right),

(56)

in terms of a discrete probability distribution over the jump operator indices
\{p_{a}\}_{a\in\bm{A}}
and where the strength
\gamma
of the dissipative terms has been renormalized accordingly.
For the purpose of implementation, we approximate the evolution under such Lindbladian through

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{M\delta t\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\approx\left(\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a_{i}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}\right)\text{where}\quad\mathcal{D}^{a}[\rho]\coloneq L^{a}\rho L^{a{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\{L^{a{\dagger}}L^{a},\rho\},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}[\rho]\coloneq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta tH}\rho\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\delta tH},

(57)

At each evolution step we randomly sample a Lindblad operator yielding a set
\{L^{a_{i}}\}_{i=1,\ldots,M}
where each of the indices
a_{i}
has been drawn with probability
p_{a_{i}}
.
The approximation in Eq. (
57
) holds upon taking this average.
Using the Taylor expansion, one can see that, on average, such sampling incurs errors that scale with
\delta t^{2}
as we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{a}p_{a}\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}[\rho]&=\rho+\delta t\mathcal{L}[\rho]+\mathcal{O}(\delta t^{2})=\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\mathcal{L}}[\rho]+\mathcal{O}(\delta t^{2}).\end{split}

(58)

While the second approximation holds upon taking the average over trajectories with randomly sampled jump operators, it is expected to hold even with a single trajectory if the number of steps
M
, at fixed evolution time
t
, is sufficiently large. The authors in
[
38
]
have shown that the individual trajectory approximates the target dynamics with an error measured in weighted
l^{2}
-distance inversely proportional to
M
(Theorem 7 in
[
38
]
).

B.1
Regularization of the integral in the operator Fourier transforms

Recall from Eq. (
8
), that the individual Lindblad operator
L^{a}
can be expressed as an operator Fourier transform via the integral

\displaystyle L^{a}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,g(t)A^{a}(t),\quad\text{where}\quad A^{a}(t)\coloneq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Ht}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Ht}.

(59)

To realize such an integral as a quantum circuit, we need to regularize it. This is achieved by restricting its domain to
[-T,T]
and discretizing it using the trapezoidal rule.
For a step size
\Delta t\coloneq T/S
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{L}^{a}&\coloneq\frac{\Delta t}{2}g_{-S}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}+\sum_{s=-S+1}^{S-1}\Delta tg_{s}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}+\frac{\Delta t}{2}g_{S}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}\\
&=\sum_{s=-S}^{S}\Delta t_{s}g_{s}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t},\end{split}

(60)

where we have defined
g_{s}\coloneq g(s\Delta t)
and
\Delta t_{s}\coloneq\Delta t
for
-S+1\leq s\leq S-1
or
\Delta t_{s}\coloneq\Delta t/2
for
s=\pm S
.

Accordingly, the Hermitian dilation operator
K^{a}
(
23
) corresponding to
L^{a}
becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{K}^{a}&\coloneq\ket{1}\bra{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\bar{L}^{a}+\ket{0}\bra{1}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\bar{L}^{a{\dagger}}=X_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\frac{\bar{L}^{a}+\bar{L}^{a{\dagger}}}{2}-\mathrm{i}Y_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\frac{\bar{L}^{a}-\bar{L}^{a{\dagger}}}{2}\\
&=\sum_{s=-S}^{S}\Delta t_{s}(\mathrm{Re}[g_{s}]X_{\mathrm{anc}}+\mathrm{Im}[g_{s}]Y_{\mathrm{anc}})\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}\\
&\eqcolon\sum_{s=-S}^{S}\bar{K}^{a}_{s},\end{split}

(61)

where
A^{a}
is assumed to be Hermitian.
Errors entailed by the discretization of the jump operators will be quantified in Sec.
B.3
, but for now we proceed with the implementation of the Lindblad evolution.

B.2
Second-order product formula for Lindblad evolution

Having defined the discretized Lindblad
\bar{L}^{a}
and dilation operators
\bar{K}^{a}
, we aim at implementing a step of dissipative evolution
\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a}}
appearing in Eq. (
57
). Recall from Eq. (
24
)
that this can be achieved, up to an error scaling as
(\delta t\gamma)^{2}
, through the unitary evolution
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}
acting on the system together with a single additional qubit:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}(\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\rho)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}]=\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a}}[\rho]+\mathcal{O}((\delta t\gamma)^{2}).

(62)

This evolution is implemented for the discretized
\bar{K}_{a}
, as a second-order product formula yielding

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\sum_{s=-S}^{S}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\approx\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}},

(63)

where we defined the ordered products
\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}O(s)=O(S)\cdots O(-S+1)O(-S)
and
\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}O(s)=O(-S)\cdots O(S-1)O(S)
.
The resulting Trotter error is given by

\displaystyle\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}=(\delta t\gamma)^{3/2}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}}c_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}}\bar{K}^{a}_{s_{1}}\bar{K}^{a}_{s_{2}}\bar{K}^{a}_{s_{3}}+\mathcal{O}((\delta t\gamma)^{2}),

(64)

with some coefficients
c_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}}
.
Furthermore, noting that
(\bra{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes I)\bar{K}^{a}_{s_{1}}\bar{K}^{a}_{s_{2}}\bar{K}^{a}_{s_{3}}(\ket{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes I)=0
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\left[\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}(\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\rho)\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}(\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\rho)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}]+\mathcal{O}((\delta t\gamma)^{2}).\end{split}

(65)

We simplify the evolution operator and estimate the evolution time required for each step of Lindblad evolution. Recalling that the evolution under a single
\bar{K}^{a}_{s}
(out of a total of
S
) takes the form,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}&=\exp\left[-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\Delta t_{s}(\mathrm{Re}[g_{s}]X_{\mathrm{anc}}+\mathrm{Im}[g_{s}]Y_{\mathrm{anc}})\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}A^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}\right]\\
&=(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t})\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\Delta t_{s}(\mathrm{Re}[g_{s}]X_{\mathrm{anc}}+\mathrm{Im}[g_{s}]Y_{\mathrm{anc}})\otimes A^{a}}}_{\eqcolon B^{a}_{s}}(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}),\end{split}

(66)

we can rewrite Eq. (
63
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}&=(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t})\underbrace{\left(\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}B^{a}_{s}(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}H\Delta t})\right)\left(\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H\Delta t})B^{a}_{s}\right)}_{\eqcolon\,V^{a}(\delta t)}(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}).\end{split}

(67)

Therefore, a single step of Lindblad evolution (
57
), which consists in a unitary part
\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}[\cdot]=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H\delta t}[\cdot]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}H\delta t}
and a dissipative part (
65
), is given by the superoperator

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{a}(\delta t)[\rho]

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t})V^{a}(\delta t)(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t})(\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}[\rho])(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t})V^{a}(\delta t)^{{\dagger}}(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t})]

\displaystyle\,=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[V^{a}(\delta t)(\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H\delta t}\rho\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}H\delta t}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t})V^{a}(\delta t)^{\dagger}]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}.

(68)

Since we sequentially apply
M
steps of evolution
\mathcal{W}^{a}(\delta t)
, the operators
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}
cancel out, except at the first and last steps.
Still, we can show that these two steps can also be discarded. For that, suppose we evolve an initial state
\rho(0)
for evolution time
M\delta t
that is larger than the mixing time
t_{\rm mix}
.
By definition of the mixing time, any initial state
\rho
evolved for a time larger than
t_{\rm mix}
approximates the Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}/\mathrm{Tr}[\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}]
. That is,

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\mathcal{D}^{a_{i}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}\right)[\rho]\overset{\text{Trotterization}}{\approx}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)\right)[\rho]\approx\sigma_{\beta}.

(69)

Taking
\rho=\mathcal{U}_{S\Delta t}[\rho(0)]
, and using the fact that
\sigma_{\beta}=\mathcal{U}_{-S\Delta t}[\sigma_{\beta}]
as
H
and
\sigma_{\beta}
commute, this implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{-S\Delta t}\circ\left(\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)\right)\circ\mathcal{U}_{S\Delta t}[\rho(0)]=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{M}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)\right)[\rho(0)]\approx\sigma_{\beta},

(70)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{a}(\delta t)[\rho]\coloneq\mathcal{U}_{-S\Delta t}\circ\mathcal{W}^{a}(\delta t)\circ\mathcal{U}_{S\Delta t}[\rho]=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[V^{a}(\delta t)(\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H\delta t}\rho\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}H\delta t})V^{a}(\delta t)^{\dagger}].

(71)

Therefore, the evolution by
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{a}[\rho]
, Eq. (
71
), induces the same Lindblad evolution as the one by
\mathcal{W}^{a}[\rho]
, Eq. (
68
), but does not require any of the unitary
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}HS\Delta t}
evolution.
The resulting quantum circuit is sketched as follows:

.

B.3
Truncation and discretization of integral

So far, the circuit implementation presented was general to the evolution under the Lindbladian of Eq. (
56
) together with the Lindblad operators obtained through the generic operator Fourier transform of Eq. (
59
). We now specialize to our protocol and specify the Lindblad operator (
59
).
Let us recall our definition of the filter function [Eq. (
53
)],

\displaystyle g(t)=\frac{1}{\pi^{3/4}\beta^{1/2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t/\beta}\mathrm{e}^{-2t^{2}/\beta^{2}},

(77)

resulting in Lindblad operators of the form

\displaystyle L^{a}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,g(t)A^{a}(t)=\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}\right)^{1/4}\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(\beta\nu+1)^{2}}{8}}A^{a}_{\nu}.

(78)

We prove the following lemma, which plays a key role in determining the complexity of the protocol.

Lemma 1
.

Let
g(t)
be the filter function defined in Eq. (
77
).
Then, provided that

\displaystyle S=\Omega\left(\beta\|H\|_{\infty}\mathrm{log}\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\right),\qquad\Delta t=\Theta\left(\frac{\beta}{\beta\|H\|_{\infty}+\sqrt{n+\mathrm{log}\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}}\right)

(79)

in (
60
), we have the regularization error

\displaystyle\|L-\bar{L}\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\prime}).

(80)

Proof.

Introducing
\bar{L}_{\infty}\coloneq\sum_{s=-\infty}^{\infty}\Delta t_{s}g_{s}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}A\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Hs\Delta t}
, we separate the discretization and truncation errors,

\displaystyle\|L-\bar{L}\|_{\infty}\leq\|L-\bar{L}_{\infty}\|_{\infty}+\|\bar{L}_{\infty}-\bar{L}\|_{\infty}.

(81)

We start with bounding the truncation error
\|\bar{L}_{\infty}-\bar{L}\|_{\infty}
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\bar{L}_{\infty}-\bar{L}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\|A\|_{\infty}}{\pi^{3/4}\beta^{1/2}}\Delta t\sum_{|s|>S}\mathrm{e}^{-2(s\Delta t/\beta)^{2}}<\frac{2\Delta t}{\pi^{3/4}\beta^{1/2}}\sum_{s=S}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2S(\Delta t/\beta)^{2}s}=\frac{2\Delta t}{\pi^{3/4}\beta^{1/2}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2(S\Delta t/\beta)^{2}}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2S(\Delta t/\beta)^{2}}}=\epsilon^{\prime},

(82)

for

\displaystyle S=\Omega\left(\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}\sqrt{\mathrm{log}\Big(\frac{\Delta t}{\sqrt{\beta}\epsilon^{\prime}}\Big)}\right),

(83)

where we used
\|A\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(1)
assuming
A
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
-local.

Next, we bound the discretization error,

\displaystyle\|L-\bar{L}_{\infty}\|_{\infty}\leq\Big\|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,g(t)A(t)-\Delta t\sum_{s=-\infty}^{\infty}g(s\Delta t)A(s\Delta t)\Big\|_{\infty}.

(84)

Using the Poisson summation formula,
3
3

3

For a function
h(\tau)
, the Poisson summation formula is given by,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=-\infty}^{\infty}h(s)=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2\pi k\tau}h(\tau).

(85)

Setting
h(s)=\Delta t\,g(s\Delta t)A(s\Delta t)
in Eq. (
85
), we arrive at Eq. (
86
).

we have,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta t\sum_{s=-\infty}^{\infty}g(s\Delta t)A(s\Delta t)&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,g(t)A(t)+\sum_{k=\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2\pi kt/\Delta t}g(t)A(t)\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,g(t)A(t)+\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}\sum_{k=\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2\pi kt/\Delta t}g(t)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu t}A_{\nu},\end{split}

(86)

leading to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|L-\bar{L}_{\infty}\|_{\infty}&\leq\frac{1}{\pi^{3/4}\beta^{1/2}}\Big\|\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}A_{\nu}\sum_{|k|>0}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2\pi kt/\Delta t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t/\beta}\mathrm{e}^{-2t^{2}/\beta^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu t}\Big\|_{\infty}\\
&=\frac{\beta^{1/2}}{2^{1/2}\pi^{1/4}}\Big\|\sum_{\nu\in B_{H}}A_{\nu}\sum_{|k|>0}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{8}\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}k-\beta\nu-1\big)^{2}}\Big\|_{\infty}\\
&\hskip-10.00002pt\overset{\nu\leq 2\|H\|_{\infty}}{\leq}\frac{\beta^{1/2}|B_{H}|\|A\|_{\infty}}{2^{1/2}\pi^{1/4}}\sum_{|k|>0}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{8}\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}k-2\beta\|H\|_{\infty}-1\big)^{2}}\\
&\leq\frac{(2\beta)^{1/2}|B_{H}|\|A\|_{\infty}}{\pi^{1/4}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{8}\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}-2\beta\|H\|_{\infty}-1\big)\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}k-2\beta\|H\|_{\infty}-1\big)}\\
&\leq\frac{(2\beta)^{1/2}|B_{H}|\|A\|_{\infty}}{\pi^{1/4}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{8}\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}-2\beta\|H\|_{\infty}-1\big)^{2}}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{8}\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}-2\beta\|H\|_{\infty}-1\big)\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}\big)}}.\end{split}

(87)

As the number of Bohr frequencies satisfies
|B_{H}|\leq 4^{n}
, it suffices to choose

\displaystyle\Delta t=\frac{2\pi\beta}{\sqrt{8\mathrm{log}\big(\frac{(2\beta)^{1/2}|B_{H}|\|A\|_{\infty}}{\pi^{1/4}\epsilon^{\prime}}\big)}+2\beta\|H\|_{\infty}+1}=\Theta\left(\frac{\beta}{\beta\|H\|_{\infty}+\sqrt{n+\mathrm{log}\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}}\right),

(88)

to ensure
\|L-\bar{L}_{\infty}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
.
Combining Eqs. (
83
) and (
88
) we obtain Eq. (
79
).
∎

Denoting as
\rho_{i}\coloneq\rho(i\delta t)
the state after
i
steps of ideal evolution, with
\rho_{0}=\rho(0)
, and by
\bar{\rho}_{i}\coloneq\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\rho_{i}
and
using Lemma
1
, we bound the effect of the regularization error on the dissipative evolution (
62
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}]-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}]\big\|_{1}\\
&=\left\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\big(\bar{\rho}_{i}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\big)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\big]\right\|_{1}\\
&\hskip-6.00006pt\overset{\eqref{eq:int_com}}{=}\Big\|\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}[\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a},\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tau\bar{K}^{a}}]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}\big]\Big\|_{1}\\
&\leq\Big\|\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}[\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a},\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tau\bar{K}^{a}}-\bar{\rho}_{i}]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}\big]\Big\|_{1}\\
&\quad+\Big\|\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}[\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a},\bar{\rho}_{i}]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}\big]\Big\|_{1}\\
&\hskip-6.00006pt\overset{\eqref{eq:b37}}{=}\mathcal{O}(\|\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a}\|_{\infty}\delta t\gamma).\end{split}

(89)

In the third line, we used the identity,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tC}A\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tC}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tB}-A=\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tau B}[B-C,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tau C}A\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tau C}]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tau B},

(90)

for non-commuting operators
A
,
B
, and
C
.
In the last inequality, we used

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}[\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a},\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tau\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tau\bar{K}^{a}}-\bar{\rho}_{i}]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}\big]\Big\|_{1}&=\mathcal{O}(\|\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a}\|_{\infty}\cdot\tau\|\bar{K}^{a}\|_{\infty}),\\
\Big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}[\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a},\bar{\rho}_{i}]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})K^{a}}\big]\Big\|_{1}&=\mathcal{O}(\|\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a}\|_{\infty}\cdot(\tau-\sqrt{\delta t\gamma})\|K^{a}\|_{\infty}),\end{split}

(91)

and
\|\bar{K}^{a}\|=\|K^{a}\|=\|A^{a}\|=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Finally, combining Eq. (
61
) and Lemma
1
together with Eq. (
89
), we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}]-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}]\big\|_{1}\\
&=\mathcal{O}(\|\bar{K}^{a}-K^{a}\|_{\infty}\delta t\gamma)=\mathcal{O}(\|\bar{L}^{a}-L^{a}\|_{\infty}\delta t\gamma)=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\prime}\delta t\gamma).\end{split}

(92)

B.4
Algorithmic errors

Combining all the previous results, we assess the resources needed for the Gibbs-state preparation protocol.
We start by relating the overall error
\epsilon
in the approximate Lindblad evolution algorithm to the total evolution time
t
, the number of performed steps
M
(or equivalently the size
\delta t
of these steps) and the error
\epsilon^{\prime}
resulting from the regularization of the integral appearing in App.
B.3
. We then provide bounds on the runtime of the Gibbs state preparation algorithm in terms of total Hamiltonian simulation time, and also the required number of steps of Hamiltonian simulation
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}H\Delta t}
, as seen in Eq. (
B.2
).

B.4.1
Lindblad simulation

The approximate Lindblad evolution incurs an error in the evolved state that is now quantified.
The trace distance between the ideal
\rho(M\delta t)\coloneq\mathrm{e}^{M\delta t\mathcal{L}}[\rho(0)]
and the approximately evolved state
\mathbb{E}_{\{a_{i}\}}\big[\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)[\rho(0)]\big]
(
68
), with the average
\mathbb{E}_{\{a_{i}\}}[\cdot]
taken over trajectories associated with the randomly sampled jump operators
\{A^{a_{1}},\dots,A^{a_{M}}\}
, is

\displaystyle\Big\|\rho(M\delta t)-\mathbb{E}_{\{a_{i}\}}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)[\rho_{0}]\Big]\Big\|_{1}.

(93)

Let us now start by recalling all the sources of errors contributing to Eq. (
93
)
using
\rho_{i}\coloneq\rho(i\delta t)
,
\rho_{0}=\rho(0)
, and
\bar{\rho}_{i}\coloneq\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\rho_{i}
as before.
We need to account for the following:

•

Randomized applications of the jump operators.

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\mathcal{L}}[\rho_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{a}[\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}[\rho_{i}]]\|_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\delta t^{2}),

(94)

according to Eq. (
58
).

•

Dilation of the dissipative evolution.

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a}}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}]\big\|_{1}=\mathcal{O}((\delta t\gamma)^{2}),

(95)

according to Eq. (
62
), and noting that the unitary evolution
\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}
does not affect the errors.

•

Truncation and discretization of the operator Fourier transform.

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}]-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}]\big\|_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\prime}\delta t\gamma)

(96)

according to Eq. (
92
).

•

Trotterization.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\Big[\Big(\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\Big)\bar{\rho}_{i}\Big(\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\Big)\Big]-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}]\Big\|_{1}\\
&=\mathcal{O}((\delta t\gamma)^{2}),\end{split}

(97)

according to Eq. (
65
).

We are now ready to combine all these errors. First, notice that at any step
i=1,\dots,M
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Big\|\rho_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{\{a_{j}\}}\Big[\prod_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{W}^{a_{j}}(\delta t)[\rho_{0}]\Big]\Big\|_{1}\\
&=\Big\|\rho_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)[\rho_{i-1}]\Big]+\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)\Big[\rho_{i-1}-\mathbb{E}_{\{a_{j}\}}\Big[\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathcal{W}^{a_{j}}(\delta t)[\rho_{0}]\Big]\Big]\Big\|_{1}\\
&\leq\Big\|\rho_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)[\rho_{i-1}]\Big]\Big\|_{1}+\Big\|\rho_{i-1}-\mathbb{E}_{\{a_{j}\}}\Big[\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathcal{W}^{a_{j}}(\delta t)[\rho_{0}]\Big]\Big\|_{1}.\end{split}

(98)

The last line is obtained through the triangle inequality and the fact that
\E_{a_{i}}[\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)]
is a quantum channel together with the contractivity of the trace distance under quantum channels. After recursive use of this inequality, we can bound the total error through

\displaystyle\Big\|\rho(M\delta t)-\mathbb{E}_{\{a_{i}\}}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)[\rho_{0}]\Big]\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\big\|\rho_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\big[\mathcal{W}^{a_{i}}(\delta t)[\rho_{i-1}]\big]\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Big(\big\|\rho_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}[\rho_{i-1}]\big]\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\big\|\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\gamma\mathcal{D}^{a_{i}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\delta t}[\rho_{i-1}]-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}]\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}K^{a}}]-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}]\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{a_{i}}\Big\|\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}\bar{\rho}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}\bar{K}^{a}}]-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{anc}}\Big[\Big(\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\Big)\bar{\rho}_{i}\Big(\prod_{s}^{\rightarrow}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\prod_{s}^{\leftarrow}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{\delta t\gamma}}{2}\bar{K}^{a}_{s}}\Big)\Big]\Big\|_{1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(M\delta t^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}M\delta t).

(99)

Hence, we can guarantee an error
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
in the prepared state, after a total Lindblad evolution time
t=M\delta t
, provided that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{t}\right),\qquad M=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\epsilon}\right).

(100)

So far, we have not specified how to implement the coherent evolutions
{\cal U}_{\delta t}
and
{\cal U}_{\Delta t}
in Eqs (
21
) and (
70
). If we employ the second-order product formula with
r_{\delta}
steps to approximately implement
{\cal U}_{\delta t}
, the error is
\mathcal{O}((\|H\|_{\infty}\delta t)^{3}/r_{\delta}^{2})
. Similarly, applying the second-order product formula to
{\cal U}_{\Delta t}
with
r_{\Delta}
steps induces the error
\mathcal{O}((\|H\|_{\infty}\Delta t)^{3}/r_{\Delta}^{2})
.
Note that for each of the
M
steps of size
\delta t
,
{\cal U}_{\Delta t}
has to be applied
4S
times in Eq. (
70
).
Thus, the error accumulated during
M
steps of the Lindblad evolution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(M\frac{(\|H\|_{\infty}\delta t)^{3}}{r_{\delta}^{2}}+MS\frac{(\|H\|_{\infty}\Delta t)^{3}}{r_{\Delta}^{2}}\right).

(101)

Choosing
r_{\delta}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{M(\|H\|_{\infty}\delta t)^{3}/\epsilon})
and
r_{\Delta}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{MS(\|H\|_{\infty}\Delta t)^{3}/\epsilon})
suffices to bound the error by
\epsilon
.

B.4.2
Gibbs state preparation

Provided a quantum system described by
H
and jump operators
\{A^{a}\}
obey the ETH (
48
), one can prepare the Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}
by simulating the approximate Lindblad dynamics (
57
) for a mixing time
t_{\rm mix}=M_{\rm mix}\delta t
of the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
(
56
).
Here, we provide the runtime of the quantum operation (
57
) to simulate the corresponding Lindblad equation in terms of the total Hamiltonian simulation time

\displaystyle M_{\rm mix}\times(\delta t+\Theta(S\Delta t))=\Theta\left(\frac{\beta t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{2}}{\epsilon}\sqrt{\mathrm{log}\frac{\beta t_{\rm mix}}{\epsilon}}\right),

(102)

to prepare an
\epsilon
-precise Gibbs state in trace distance. For the simulation time corresponding to the dissipative part, we used
S
and
\Delta t
satisfying Lemma
1
with
\epsilon^{\prime}
given by Eq. (
100
).
The resulting circuit, from Eqs. (
B.2
) and (
B.2
), uses a total of

\displaystyle M_{\rm mix}\times\Theta(S)=\Theta\left(\frac{\beta t_{\mathrm{mix}}^{2}\|H\|_{\infty}}{\epsilon}\mathrm{log}\frac{\beta t_{\rm mix}}{\epsilon}\right)

(103)

applications of
B_{s}(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}H\Delta t})
and
(I_{\mathrm{anc}}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H\Delta t})B_{s}
.

For the purpose of the error analysis conducted in Sec.
7
, we collect all the sources of algorithmic error along the Lindblad simulation for time
t_{\rm mix}=M_{\rm mix}\delta t
.
While we stated the runtime without specifying a Hamiltonian simulation algorithm in Eq. (
102
), we here adopt the second-order Trotter formula as discussed around Eq. (
101
) to be aligned with the numerical simulations presented in the main text.
Adding Eqs. (
94
), (
95
),
(
96
),(
97
), and (
101
), we obtain the total algorithmic error, summarized below.

Proposition 1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the Lindbladian given by Eq. (
56
) with the Lindblad operators (
78
), mixing time
t_{\mathrm{mix}}
and inverse temperature
\beta
.
Let
|B_{H}|
be the number of Bohr frequencies for the Hamiltonian
H
.
Moreover, let
\delta t
be the evolution step size in Eq. (
19
),
\Delta t
and
T
be the OFT discretization step and integral range limit appearing in Eq (
25
),
and
r_{\delta}
and
r_{\Delta}
as given in Eq. (
101
).
The total algorithmic error of the algorithm in Thm.
1
is given by

\displaystyle t_{\rm mix}\times\mathcal{O}\left(\delta t+\frac{\Delta t}{\sqrt{\beta}}\mathrm{e}^{-2(T/\beta)^{2}}+\sqrt{\beta}|B_{H}|\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{8}\big(2\pi\frac{\beta}{\Delta t}-2\beta\|H\|_{\infty}-1\big)^{2}}+\frac{\delta t^{2}}{r_{\delta}^{2}}+\frac{T\Delta t^{2}}{\delta t\,r_{\Delta}^{2}}\right).

(104)

For
\epsilon^{\prime}
in the truncation and discretization error (
96
), we used Eqs. (
82
) and (
87
), resulting in the second and third terms in Eq (
104
).
In Eq. (
27
) of the main text, we drop the second last term
\propto\delta t^{2}
as this is subleading to the first term
\propto\delta t
, and set
r_{\Delta}=1
to align with the setup adopted in the main text.

Appendix C
Convergence of Lindblad dynamics under the ETH

This appendix details the convergence analysis of the Lindblad dynamics towards the Gibbs state, assuming that the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH) holds.
For this, we follow the strategy of
[
60
]
and compute the spectral gap of the ETH-averaged Lindbladian
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
and relate it, via a bound on the channel distance between
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
and the actual Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
, to the mixing time of
\mathcal{L}
.
Throughout this appendix we set
\gamma_{a}=1/|{\bm{A}}|
in Eqs. (
3
) and (
51
).

In Sec.
C.1
we restate the ETH, and discuss the energy scales at play together with the assumptions made.
In Sec.
C.2
we introduce the density of states and summarize the properties relevant for our subsequent derivations.
Following this, we compute the spectral gap of the averaged Lindbladian
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
and the distance between
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}
in Secs.
C.3
and
C.4
.
Based on these derivations, we then prove in Sec.
C.5
our final mixing time bound for the actual Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
[Eq. (
17
)], and the closeness of the steady state of
\mathcal{L}
to the target Gibbs state [Eq. (
18
)],
which stated in the following theorem.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose a Hamiltonian
H
and a set of Hermitian operators
\{A^{a}\}_{a=1,\dots,|{\bf A}|}
obey the ETH,

\bra{E_i}A^{a}\ket{E_j}=\mathcal{A}(E_{i})\delta_{ij}+D(E_{ij})^{-\frac{1}{2}}f(E_{ij},\nu_{ij})R_{ij}
,
with
E_{ij}\coloneq(E_{i}+E_{j})/2
, such that
E_{i}=E_{ii}
, and
\nu_{ij}\coloneq E_{i}-E_{j}
.
Furthermore suppose the function
f(E,\nu)
satisfies Assumption
1
and the density of states
D(E_{ij})
satisfies Assumptions
2
and
3
. Then, for an
n
-qubit Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}
and
\epsilon>0
, there is a Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
admitting
\rho_{\infty}
as a steady state such that,

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\infty}-\sigma_{\beta}\|_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\epsilon+\frac{n\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{|\bm{A}|}}\left(\beta\|H\|_{\infty}+\mathrm{log}(1/\epsilon)\right)\right)

(105)

with high probability.
Moreover, the dynamics
\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}}
prepares a state
\epsilon
-close to
\rho_{\infty}
in trace distance for any
t\geq t_{\rm mix}
with

\displaystyle t_{\rm mix}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(n\beta^{2}\big(\beta\|H\|_{\infty}+\mathrm{log}(1/\epsilon)\big)\right).

(106)

We prove this theorem in the remainder of App.
C
. See Eqs. (
186
) and (
196
) for the final results.

C.1
Eigenstate thermalization hypothesis

The ETH, originally proposed by Srednicki
[
48
]
, hypothesizes that, for a Hamiltonian
H
, the matrix elements of a local operator
A
in the eigenbasis
\{\ket{E_i}\}
of
H
is expressed as [Eq. (
11
)]

\displaystyle A_{ij}\coloneq\bra{E_i}A\ket{E_j}=\mathcal{A}(E_{i})\delta_{ij}+\frac{f(E_{ij},\nu_{ij})}{\sqrt{D(E_{ij})}}R_{ij},

(107)

with
E_{ij}\coloneq(E_{i}+E_{j})/2
, such that
E_{i}=E_{ii}
, and
\nu_{ij}\coloneq E_{i}-E_{j}
. We will also sometimes use
D_{ij}\coloneq D(E_{ij})
and
f_{ij}\coloneq f(E_{ij},\nu_{ij})
.
Both
\mathcal{A}(E)
and
f(E,\nu)
are smooth functions of
E
and
\nu
.
We defer the discussion of the properties of the density of states
D(E)
to Sec.
C.2
.

R
is a Hermitian matrix with entries
R_{ij}
whose real and imaginary parts are independent random variables that satisfy
\mathbb{E}_{R}[R_{ij}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}_{R}[|R_{ij}|^{2}]=1
. Furthermore, its diagonal entries are set to
R_{ii}=0
for all
i
.

Given the ETH ansatz (
107
) and properties of the random variables
R_{ij}
, one can verify several identities that will be useful later on when performing averages over entries of
R
. First, we have

\mathbb{E}_{R}[A_{ij}A_{kl}^{*}]=\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}\mathcal{A}(E_{i})\mathcal{A}(E_{k})+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\frac{f(E_{ij},\nu_{ij})}{\sqrt{D(E_{ij})}}.

(108)

Furthermore, recalling that
A_{\nu}=\sum_{E_{i}-E_{j}=\nu}\Pi_{j}A\Pi_{i}
, with
\Pi_{i}
the projector onto the Hamiltonian’s eigenspace with energy
E_{i}
, one can verify that

\mathbb{E}_{R}[A_{\nu_{1}}\rho A^{\dagger}_{\nu_{2}}]=\delta_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{R}[A_{\nu_{1}}\rho A^{\dagger}_{\nu_{1}}],\quad\textrm{and }\;\mathbb{E}_{R}\left[\{A^{\dagger}_{\nu_{1}}A^{\dagger}_{\nu_{1}},\rho\}\right]=\delta_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{R}\left[\{A^{\dagger}_{\nu_{1}}A^{\dagger}_{\nu_{1}},\rho\}\right].

(109)

To proceed with the concrete calculation of the mixing time, we make a couple of simplifying assumptions on the non-universal function
f(E,\nu)
.

Assumption 1
.

The function
f(E,\nu)
in Eq. (
107
) satisfies the following properties.

(a)

f(E,\nu)=f(\nu)
is independent of
E
, and supported on
\nu\in[-\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}},\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}]
, where it is flat.

(b)

The width of the support scales with
\beta
as
\Delta_{\rm RMT}=\Theta(1/\beta)
, and the function takes an
(n,\beta)
-independent constant value
f_{0}
on the support.

To motivate the assumptions we note that an operator
A
, such that
\|A^{2}\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, obeys
\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma_{\beta}A^{2}]\leq\|A^{2}\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(1)
. With the ETH, one can express the trace in the integral form,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\sigma_{\beta}A^{2}]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta\nu/2}|f(\nu)|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1).

(110)

To ensure that the integral converges to a finite value, we need
|f(\nu)|\xrightarrow{\nu\to\infty}o(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\nu/4})

[
48
,
100
]
.
Assumption
1
(b)
provides a simple restriction on
f(\nu)
so that this constraint is met. While we made specific choices of
\Delta_{\rm RMT}
and
|f(\nu)|
, any polynomial dependence of them on
n
also leads to a polynomial upper bound on the mixing time. In the following, we shall provide computational resources with and without Assumption
1
(b)
.

\nu
|f(\nu)|^{2}
\Delta_{\text{E}}=\Theta(\Delta_{\text{RMT}})
\eta_{\nu}^{2}
-\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}}{\sqrt{2}}=\Theta(\Delta_{\text{RMT}})
\Delta_{\text{RMT}}=\Theta(1/\beta)

Figure 9:

Squared Gaussian filter function
\eta_{\nu}^{2}
(
55
) from the operator Fourier transform and the function
|f(\nu)|^{2}
in the off-diagonal ETH (
11
). The
relationship between their supports
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}=\sqrt{2}/\beta=\Theta(\Delta_{\text{RMT}})
guarantees significant overlap between the
two functions.

We illustrate the relevant scales under Assumption
1
(b)
in Fig.
9
.
Given the above condition on
|f(\nu)|
, we choose the width of our filter function
\eta_{\nu}
(
55
) to be
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}=\Theta(\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}})=\Theta(1/\beta)
.
This, in turn, leads to the filter being peaked at
\nu=-\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}/2=-\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}/\sqrt{2}
.
Figure
9
illustrates that there is a significant overlap between the supports of
\eta_{\nu}^{2}
and
|f(\nu)|^{2}
, which guarantees efficient transition of states upon applications of the jump operators.
Quantitatively, we can derive upper and lower bounds on the overlap integrals,

\displaystyle\Gamma\coloneq\int_{0}^{\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\eta_{-\nu}^{2}|f(\nu)|^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Gamma_{\infty}\coloneq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\eta_{\nu}^{2}|f(\nu)|^{2},

(111)

which will show up frequently in this appendix. First, we can relate these two integrals through

\Gamma\leq\Gamma_{\infty}\leq 2\Gamma,

(112)

where we used the fact that
f
is only supported on
\nu\in[-\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}},\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}]
, from Assumption
1
(a)
, and the profile of
\eta_{\nu}
(
55
) that has most of its mass on the range
[-\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}},0]
.
Hence it is sufficient to derive bounds for
\Gamma
.
For its upper bound, we insert Eq. (
55
) and obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma\leq\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\int_{-\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}^{\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\mathrm{e}^{-((\beta\nu)^{2}+1)/4}\mathrm{e}^{\beta\nu/2}|f(\nu)|^{2}\leq\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\int_{-\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}^{\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta\nu/2}|f(\nu)|^{2}\overset{\eqref{eq:f_scale_derivation}}{=}\mathcal{O}(\beta),\end{split}

(113)

On the other hand, Assumption
1
(b)
allows one to lower bound the overlap,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma\geq\frac{\beta\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta\Delta_{\rm RMT})^{2}/4}\mathrm{e}^{-1/4}}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\nu\,|f(\nu)|^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta\Delta_{\rm RMT})^{2}/4}\beta\mathrm{e}^{-1/4}}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}|f_{0}|^{2}=\Theta(1),\end{split}

(114)

using
\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}=\Theta(1/\beta)
in the last equality.

C.2
Density of states

We introduce the density of states

\displaystyle D(E)=\sum_{E_{i}\in{\rm spec}[H]}\tilde{\delta}(E-E_{i}).

(115)

Here
\tilde{\delta}(E-E_{i})
is a smeared delta function ensuring that

D(E)
is a smooth function of
E
.
The following assumption on the density of states guarantees that the ratios
appearing in the conductance calculations in Sec.
C.3
are of order 1.

Assumption 2

(Bounded ratio of density of states)
.

The ratio of densities of states is uniformly bounded such that for all
|E-E^{\prime}|\leq\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}

\frac{D(E)}{D(E^{\prime})}\leq R_{D}=\Theta(1).

(116)

We note that in
[
60
]
, the spectrum of the Hamiltonian is truncated to exclude pathological cases
that would invalidate this bound on the ratio close to the extremal values of the spectrum.

The normalized density of Gibbs states is defined by

\displaystyle D_{\beta}(E)\propto\mathrm{e}^{-\beta E}D(E)

(117)

with the normalization taken to satisfy
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}ED_{\beta}(E)=1
. We require that the bulk of the density of Gibbs states is lower-bounded by a characteristic scale
1/\Delta_{\text{spec}}
and that the tails decay sufficiently fast. This is captured by the following assumption.

Assumption 3

(Density of Gibbs states)
.

The (normalized) density of Gibbs states
D_{\beta}(E)
, defined in Eq. (
117
), satisfies the following properties.

(a)

There exists an interval
[E_{\mathrm{L}},E_{\mathrm{R}}]
that
contains more than half the weight, such that
\int_{E_{\mathrm{L}}}^{E_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrm{d}ED_{\beta}(E)\geq 1/2
, and for all
E\in[E_{\mathrm{L}},E_{\mathrm{R}}]
we have

D_{\beta}(E)\geq D_{\beta}^{\rm min}=\Omega(\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}^{-1}).

(118)

(b)

The right tail
[E_{\mathrm{R}},\infty)
and left tail

(-\infty,E_{\mathrm{L}}]
decay such that for all
E\in[E_{\mathrm{R}},\infty)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{E}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}D_{\beta}(E^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}D_{\beta}(E)\right)

(119)

and for all
E\in(-\infty,E_{\mathrm{L}}]
we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{E}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}D_{\beta}(E^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}D_{\beta}(E)\right).

(120)

To gain intuition about these assumptions, it is instructive to think of the density of states as a Gaussian

\displaystyle D(E)\propto\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(E-E_{\infty})^{2}}{2\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}^{2}}},

(121)

where
E_{\infty}\coloneq\mathrm{Tr}[H]/2^{n}
is the energy at infinite temperature. Then the density
of Gibbs states is a Gaussian shifted by
-\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}

\displaystyle D_{\beta}(E)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}^{2}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(E-E_{\infty}+\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}^{2})^{2}}{2\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}^{2}}}.

(122)

Figure
10
illustrates those densities.
It can be shown that those Gaussian densities fulfill Assumptions
2
and
3

[
60
]
.
For instance, the density (
122
) indeed obeys Assumption
3
(b)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{E}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}D_{\beta}(E^{\prime})&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}}}\int_{E}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(E^{\prime}-E_{\infty}+\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2})^{2}}{2\Delta_{\text{spec}}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{|E-E_{\infty}+\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}|/\sqrt{2\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{e}^{-u^{2}}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}w}\int_{w}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\,2u\mathrm{e}^{-u^{2}}\qquad\left(w=|E-E_{\infty}+\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}|/\sqrt{2\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}}\,\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}w}\int_{w^{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{e}^{-u}\\
&=\frac{\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}}{|E-E_{\infty}+\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}|}D_{\beta}(E)\\
&\leq\Delta_{\text{spec}}D_{\beta}(E).\end{split}

(123)

The last line follows from
\Delta_{\text{spec}}\leq|E-E_{\infty}+\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}|
in the tail
E\geq E_{\mathrm{R}}
.

E
E_{\infty}
E_{\infty}-\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}
D(E)
D_{\beta}(E)
\Delta_{\text{spec}}

Figure 10:
Example of the density of states
D(E)
and density of the
Gibbs state
D_{\beta}(E)
that we consider.

C.3
Spectral gap of
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}

In this section, we derive a lower bound on the spectral gap of the Lindbladian
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
under the ETH average. After working out the action of the average Lindbladian onto eigenstates projectors, in Sec.
C.3.1
, we proceed in two steps: First, in Sec.
C.3.2
, we show that the average Lindbladian
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
can be mapped to a classical Markov chain admitting the Gibbs state as its exact steady state.
In the second step, in Sec.
C.3.3
we work out bounds on the conductance of the Markov chain, which allows us to bounds its spectral gap through Cheeger’s inequality.
Subsequently, in Sec.
C.4
, we derive an upper bound on the distance between the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
and the averaged one
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
, which implies that the steady state
\rho_{\infty}
of
\mathcal{L}
is close to the Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}
.

C.3.1
Action of the averaged Lindbladian

We wish to evaluate the action of the averaged Lindbladian
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
, with
\mathcal{L}
given in Eq. (
56
), onto any of the operators
\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}
.
Setting
\gamma_{a}=1/|{\bm{A}}|
in Eq. (
51
), or equivalently
p_{a}=1/|{\bm{A}}|
and
\gamma=1
in Eq. (
56
), together with our choice of jump operators in Eq. (
54
), we can write the dissipative part of our Lindbladian as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\rho]=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\Big(L^{a}\rho L^{a{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\{L^{a{\dagger}}L^{a},\rho\}\Big)=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\eta_{\nu_{1}}\eta_{\nu_{2}}\Big(A^{a}_{\nu_{1}}\rho A^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\{A^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu_{1}}A^{a}_{\nu_{2}},\rho\}\Big).

(124)

Taking an average over the random matrices elements appearing in Eq. (
107
), and using Eq. (
109
), leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{D}[\rho]=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\sum_{\nu}\eta_{\nu}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{R}\Big[A^{a}_{\nu}\rho A^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\{A^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}A^{a}_{\nu},\rho\}\Big],

(125)

that has the form of the Davies generator
[
101
,
102
]
.
Let us define
D_{ij}\coloneq D(E_{ij})
together with
\eta_{ij}\coloneq\eta_{\nu_{ij}}
and
f^{a}_{ij}\coloneq f^{a}(\nu_{ij})
. Recall that
\nu_{ij}=E_{i}-E_{j}
. Using Eq. (
107
), we can see that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\nu}\eta_{\nu}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{R}[A^{a}_{\nu}\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}A_{\nu}^{a\dagger}]=\eta_{0}^{2}\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{i})\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{j})\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}+\delta_{ij}\sum_{k\neq i}\frac{\eta_{ki}^{2}|f^{a}_{ki}|^{2}}{D_{ki}}\ket{E_k}\bra{E_k}.\end{split}

(126)

Similarly, for the second term appearing in Eq. (
125
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\nu}\eta_{\nu}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{R}[\{A_{\nu}^{a\dagger}A_{\nu}^{a},\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}\}]=&\left(\eta_{0}^{2}\left(\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{i})^{2}+\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{j})^{2}\right)+\sum_{\nu\neq 0}\eta_{\nu}^{2}\left(\frac{|f^{a}_{i(i+\nu)}|^{2}}{D_{i(i+\nu)}}+\frac{|f^{a}_{j(j+\nu)}|^{2}}{D_{j(j+\nu)}}\right)\right)\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}.\end{split}

(127)

Finally, we note that
[H,\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}]=(E_{i}-E_{j})\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}
.

Putting everything together we can evaluate the action of
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
onto any of the
\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}
operators. For the case where
i=j
we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}[\ket{E_i}\bra{E_i}]=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\eta_{ji}^{2}|f^{a}_{ji}|^{2}}{D_{ji}}(\ket{E_j}\bra{E_j}-\ket{E_i}\bra{E_i}).

(128)

On the other hand for
i\neq j
, after some reordering, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}[\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}]=\left(-\mathrm{i}(E_{i}-E_{j})-\frac{1}{2|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\left(\eta_{0}^{2}\left(\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{i})-\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{j})\right)^{2}+\sum_{m\neq i}\eta_{mi}^{2}\frac{|f^{a}_{im}|^{2}}{D_{im}}+\sum_{l\neq j}\eta_{lj}^{2}\frac{|f^{a}_{jl}|^{2}}{D_{jl}}\right)\right)\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}.

(129)

In the following, we will often need to bound the terms
\sum_{k\neq i}\frac{\eta_{ki}^{2}|f_{ki}|^{2}}{D_{ki}}
, for a fixed
i
, appearing in Eqs. (
128
) and (
129
). To do so, we first approximate the sums as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\neq i}\frac{\eta_{ki}^{2}|f_{ki}|^{2}}{D_{ki}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}E_{k}\,D(E_{k})\eta_{ki}^{2}\frac{|f_{ki}|^{2}}{D\left(E_{ki}\right)}=\int_{E_{i}-\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}^{E_{i}+\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}E_{k}\,D(E_{k})\eta_{ki}^{2}\frac{|f_{ki}|^{2}}{D\left(E_{ki}\right)}.

(130)

We first replaced the sum by an integral
that holds for a smooth function
g(E)
.
Then, given that
f(\nu)
is supported on the interval
[-\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}},\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}]
, we restricted the integral’s interval.
Finally, using the fact that the ratio of densities is bounded through Assumption (
2
) and the definition of
\Gamma
in Eq. (
111
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma}{R_{D}}\leq\sum_{k\neq i}\frac{\eta_{ki}^{2}|f_{ki}|^{2}}{D_{ki}}\leq R_{D}\Gamma.

(131)

C.3.2
Mapping to a classical Markov chain

Here, we show that the average Lindbladian
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
defines the generator of a classical Markov chain on the spectrum of
H
.
The transition rates
q_{i\to j}
between eigenstate projectors
\ket{E_i}\bra{E_i}
are readily obtained from Eq. (
128
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}q_{i\to j}&\coloneq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\ket{E_j}\bra{E_j}\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}[\ket{E_i}\bra{E_i}]\right]=\frac{\eta_{ji}^{2}|f_{ji}|^{2}}{D_{ji}}-\delta_{ij}\sum_{k\neq i}\frac{\eta_{ki}^{2}|f_{ki}|^{2}}{D_{ki}}\,,\end{split}

(132)

where for the sake of simplicity we assumed that
f^{a}=f
for any
a\in{\bm{A}}
. Moving to the off-diagonal elements
\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}
, we saw from Eq. (
129
) that these were eigenvectors of
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
.
From Eq. (
131
), the minimum (in magnitude) of the real part of these eigenvalues is lower bounded through

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta^{\rm off}&\coloneq\min_{ij}\frac{1}{2|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\left(\eta_{0}^{2}\left(\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{i})-\mathcal{A}^{a}(E_{j})\right)^{2}+\sum_{m\neq i}\eta_{mi}^{2}\frac{|f^{a}_{im}|^{2}}{D_{im}}+\sum_{l\neq j}\eta_{lj}^{2}\frac{|f^{a}_{jl}|^{2}}{D_{jl}}\right)\geq\frac{\Gamma}{R_{D}}.\end{split}

(133)

Under the time evolution with
\mathrm{e}^{t\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}}
, the imaginary part of the eigenvalues results in a phase, while the real part leads to a decay of the amplitude on the off-diagonal operators
\ket{E_i}\bra{E_j}
.
Later on, we will discuss the timescales of such decay, but for now focus on
the classical Markov chain defined by Eq. (
132
), which is just the restriction of
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
to eigenstate projectors. This converges to the Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}
, since
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
is
\sigma_{\beta}
-DB, as discussed in Sec.
3.2
,
and the mixing time of the chain is controlled by the transition rates (
132
).

We shift and rescale the transition rates
q_{i\to j}
to obtain a stochastic transition matrix of the classical Markov chain:

\displaystyle P_{i\to j}\coloneq\frac{q_{i\to j}+r\delta_{ij}}{r},\quad\text{with}\;r\coloneq\max_{i}\sum_{k\neq i}\frac{\eta_{ki}^{2}|f_{ki}|^{2}}{D_{ki}}.

(134)

To see that with such choice
P_{i\to j}
is indeed stochastic
4
4

4

We say
P_{i\to j}
is stochastic if it satisfies
\sum_{j}P_{i\to j}=1
for all
i
and any
P_{i\to j}\geq 0
.

we first note that the transition rates (
132
) satisfy
\sum_{j}q_{i\to j}=0
. Hence,
\sum_{j}P_{i\to j}=1
for any choice of
r>0
. It remains to show that the chosen
r
ensures that any
P_{i\to j}\geq 0
.
This can be seen by noting that
r
is equal to
-\max_{i}q_{i\to i}
and that the
q_{i\to i}
are the only negative transition rates. Finally, according to Eq. (
131
), we readily see that
r
is bounded through

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma}{R_{D}}\leq r\leq R_{D}\Gamma,

(135)

Given the spectral gap
\Delta_{P}
of the Markov chain with transition matrix
P
, the gap of the diagonal part of
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
is
\Delta^{\rm diag}=r\Delta_{P}
, and
we now wish to bound
\Delta_{P}
.
To this end, we relate the gap of the Markov chain with its conductance
\Phi
defined as

\displaystyle\Phi(S)\coloneq\frac{Q(S,\bar{S})}{\pi_{S}},\qquad Q(S_{1},S_{2})\coloneq\sum_{i\in S_{1},j\in S_{2}}\pi_{i}P_{i\to j}.

(136)

Here,
S\subset\Spec(H)
refers to a subset of the spectrum of
H
and
\bar{S}
to its complement. We further defined the probability
\pi_{S}\coloneq\sum_{i\in S}\pi_{i}

for the stationary distribution
\pi_{i}
of the Markov chain. This stationary distribution
\pi_{i}
satisfies the detailed balance condition given by
\pi_{i}P_{i\to j}=\pi_{j}P_{j\to i}
for any
i,j\in\Spec(H)
.
Defining the minimum conductance (also called bottleneck ratio) as

\displaystyle\phi\coloneq\min_{\pi_{S}\leq 1/2}\frac{Q(S,\bar{S})}{\pi_{S}},

(137)

the spectral gap
\Delta_{P}
is bounded via Cheeger’s inequality as

\displaystyle\frac{\phi^{2}}{2}\leq\Delta_{P}\leq 2\phi.

(138)

C.3.3
Conductance calculation

In the following we aim at bounding the conductance (
137
). For that, we introduce a set of non-overlapping energy intervals
\{S_{i}\}_{i}
, each of which has size
\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}
as sketched in Fig.
11
. The intervals
S_{i}
are labeled in increasing order of energies, i.e.,
E_{i}<E_{i+1}
for any
E_{i}\in S_{i}
and
E_{i+1}\in S_{i+1}
.
Furthermore, we assume for now that the subset
S
appearing in the definition of the conductance has the form
S_{\ell}\cup S_{\ell+1}\cup\dots\cup S_{r}
supported on the energy interval
[E_{\ell},E_{r}]
, such that
\pi_{S}\leq 1/2
.
That is, we assume that
S
is contiguous over the energy spectrum. When
S
is not contiguous, we can decompose it into contiguous subsets and combine their conductance bounds, as discussed later.
We divide the analysis of the conductance into four cases
[
60
]
that cover all the possible locations of
E_{l}
and
E_{r}
with respect to the interval
[E_{L},E_{R}]
that was defined in Assumption
3
.

S
\bar{S}
\bar{S}
E_{\ell}
E_{r}
E_{\mathrm{L}}
E_{\mathrm{R}}
S_{\ell-1}
S_{\ell}
P_{\ell\rightarrow\ell-1}
P_{r\rightarrow r+1}
S_{r}
S_{r+1}
E
E_{\infty}-\beta\Delta_{\text{spec}}^{2}
D_{\beta}(E)
\Delta_{\text{RMT}}
\Delta_{\text{spec}}

Figure 11:

Sketch of energy intervals, transitions, and density of states that contribute to the conductance from
S
to
\bar{S}
.

Case
E_{r}\in[E_{\mathrm{L}}+\Delta_{\rm RMT},E_{\mathrm{R}}-\Delta_{\rm RMT}]
.

The conductance is lower-bounded with Assumptions
2
and
3
as

\displaystyle\Phi(S)

\displaystyle\geq 2Q(S,\bar{S})\geq 2(\pi_{S_{r}}P_{r\to r+1}+\pi_{S_{\ell}}P_{\ell\to\ell-1})\geq 2\pi_{S_{r}}P_{r\to r+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r}\sum_{E_{r}\in S_{r},E_{r+1}\in S_{r+1}}\pi_{E_{r}}\eta_{r,r+1}^{2}\frac{|f_{r,r+1}|^{2}}{D_{r,r+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r}\int_{E_{r-1}}^{E_{r}}\mathrm{d}E\,D_{\beta}(E)\int_{E_{r}}^{E_{r+1}}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}\,D(E^{\prime})\eta_{E^{\prime}-E}^{2}\frac{|f(E-E^{\prime})|^{2}}{D\left(\frac{E+E^{\prime}}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\hskip-6.99997pt\overset{\text{Ass.\penalty\ \ref{as:dos_ratio}}}{\geq}\frac{2}{rR_{D}}\int_{E_{r-1}}^{E_{r}}\mathrm{d}E\,D_{\beta}(E)\int_{E_{r}}^{E_{r+1}}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}\,\eta_{E^{\prime}-E}^{2}|f(E-E^{\prime})|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-11.99998pt\overset{\text{Ass.\penalty\ \ref{as:dos_gibbs}\ref{as:dos_gibbs_bulk}}}{\geq}\frac{2D_{\beta}^{\rm min}}{rR_{D}}\int_{E_{r-1}}^{E_{r}}\mathrm{d}E\int_{E_{r}}^{E_{r+1}}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}\,\eta_{E^{\prime}-E}^{2}|f(E-E^{\prime})|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-11.99998pt\overset{\nu=E^{\prime}-E}{=}\frac{2D_{\beta}^{\rm min}}{rR_{D}}\int_{E_{r-1}}^{E_{r}}\mathrm{d}E\,\int_{E_{r}-E}^{E_{r+1}-E}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\eta_{\nu}^{2}|f_{\nu}|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-11.99998pt\overset{\mu=E_{r}-E}{=}\frac{2D_{\beta}^{\rm min}}{rR_{D}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{\text{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\mu\int_{\mu}^{\Delta_{\text{RMT}}+\mu}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\eta_{\nu}^{2}|f_{\nu}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2D_{\beta}^{\rm min}}{rR_{D}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{\text{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\mu\int_{0}^{\Delta_{\text{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\eta_{\nu}^{2}|f_{\nu}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2D_{\beta}^{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}{rR_{D}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{\text{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\nu\,\eta_{-\nu}^{2}|f_{\nu}|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-6.99997pt\overset{\eqref{eq:spectral_gap_of_average_L_rshift_upper}}{=}\Omega\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}}\right).

(139)

The derivations of such bounds only account for energy-increasing transitions, as seen in the first line of the derivations.

Case
E_{\ell}\in[E_{\mathrm{L}}+\Delta_{\rm RMT},E_{\mathrm{R}}-\Delta_{\rm RMT}]
.

Following similar steps, the conductance is lower-bounded as

\displaystyle\Phi(S)

\displaystyle\geq 2\pi_{S_{\ell}}P_{\ell\to\ell-1}=\frac{2}{r}\sum_{E_{\ell}\in S_{\ell},E_{\ell-1}\in S_{\ell-1}}\pi_{E_{\ell}}\eta_{\ell-1,\ell}^{2}\frac{|f_{\ell,\ell-1}|^{2}}{D\big(\frac{E_{\ell}+E_{\ell-1}}{2}\big)}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\Delta_{\text{RMT}}}{\Delta_{\text{spec}}}\right).

(140)

Note that the energy-increasing conductance bound (
139
) is smaller than the energy-decreasing one (
140
).

Case
E_{r}\in(-\infty,E_{\mathrm{L}}]
.

Under Assumption
3
,
Q(S,\bar{S})
is lower-bounded as,

\displaystyle Q(S,\bar{S})

\displaystyle\geq\pi_{S_{r}}P_{r\to r+1}\geq\frac{1}{r}\min_{E_{r}\in S_{r}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}D_{\beta}(E_{r})\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\mathrm{d}\nu\eta_{-\nu}^{2}|f(\nu)|^{2}.

(141)

and

\displaystyle\pi_{S}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{E_{r}}\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}D_{\beta}(E^{\prime})={\cal O}\big(\Delta_{\rm spec}D_{\beta}(E_{r})\big).

(142)

Thus, the conductance is bounded as

\displaystyle\Phi(S)=\frac{Q(S,\bar{S})}{\pi_{S}}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}}\right).

(143)

Case
E_{\ell}\in[E_{\mathrm{R}},\infty)
.

Following similar steps as for the previous case leads to the same lower bound (
140
).

To deal with unions of contiguous energy sets, let us first consider the case with two such sets,
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, that are not contiguous to each other (and thus do not overlap). We wish to relate the conductance
\Phi(S_{12}\coloneq S_{1}\cup S_{2})
to the individual conductances
\Phi(S_{1})
and
\Phi(S_{2})
. To do so, we note that given Assumption
1
(a)
, transitions
P_{i\to j}
between any
i\in S_{1}
to any
j\in S_{2}
are suppressed such that
Q(S_{12},\bar{S}_{12})=Q(S_{1},\bar{S}_{1})+Q(S_{2},\bar{S}_{2})
. Hence

\Phi(S_{12})=\frac{Q(S_{1},\bar{S}_{1})+Q(S_{2},\bar{S}_{2})}{\pi_{S_{1}}+\pi_{S_{2}}}\geq\min\{\Phi(S_{1}),\Phi(S_{2})\}.

(144)

This can extended to unions of more than two non-contiguous sets, and shows that taking such a union cannot decrease the conductance.
Overall, accounting for all the cases studied, we get that the conductance is lower-bounded as

\displaystyle\Phi(S)\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}}\right),

(145)

which thus gives the minimum conductance, i.e.,

\displaystyle\phi\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}}\right).

(146)

Therefore, using Cheeger’s inequality (
138
) together with the relationship between the classical Markov chain and the diagonal part of the averaged Lindbladian (
134
), we obtain the lower-bound for the spectral gap

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm diag}\geq\frac{r\phi^{2}}{2}=\Omega\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}^{2}\Gamma}{\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}^{2}}\right).

(147)

Using
\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}=\Theta(1/\beta)
(Assumption
1
(b)
)
and the lower-bound on the overlap integrals (
114
),
we get

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm diag}=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{n\beta^{2}}\right),

(148)

Here, we further used a standard scaling of the spectral width of a local Hamiltonian,
\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})

[
103
,
104
,
105
]
.
Finally, comparing the scaling of Eq. (
148
) to one of the eigenvalues of the off-diagonal part of the averaged Lindbladian (
133
), we conclude that
\Delta^{\rm diag}
provides the lower bound of the spectral gap of the average Lindbladian,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\beta\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{RMT}}^{2}\Gamma}{\Delta_{\mathrm{spec}}^{2}}\right)\overset{\text{Ass.}\ref{as:f_ETH}\ref{as:f_ETH_b}}{=}\Omega\left(\frac{1}{n\beta^{2}}\right).

(149)

Recall that this spectral gap bound is derived under the assumption of ETH (
107
) and Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
.

C.4
Concentration: upper bound of
\|\mathcal{L}-\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2\to 2}

We derive an upper bound on the operator distance between the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
and its ETH average
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{L}
.
A convenient distance measure for our purposes is the weighted induced quantum channel norm
\|\cdot\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2\to 2}

[
56
]
, which is defined by

\displaystyle\|{\cal C}\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2\to 2}\coloneq\max_{X}\frac{\|{\cal C}[X]\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2}}{\|X\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2}},\quad\text{with}\quad\|X\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2}\coloneq\sqrt{\expectationvalue{X, X}_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1}}}\overset{\eqref{eq:DB_inner_product_general}}{=}\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}[X^{\dagger}\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/2}X\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/2}]},

(150)

for a quantum channel
{\cal C}
and the maximization is performed over Hermitian operators
X
.
Later, this norm will allow us to bound the trace distance between the steady states
\rho_{\infty}
and
\sigma_{\beta}
.

Note that the ETH average does not affect the system Hamiltonian, such that we can simplify

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{E}_{R}{\mathcal{L}}\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2\to 2}=\|{\cal D}-\mathbb{E}_{R}{\cal D}\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2\to 2}.

(151)

where
\mathcal{D}
is the dissipative part of
\mathcal{L}
, which form we recall here:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\rho]=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\Big(L^{a}\rho L^{a{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\{L^{a{\dagger}}L^{a},\rho\}\Big)=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\eta_{\nu_{1}}\eta_{\nu_{2}}\Big(A^{a}_{\nu_{1}}\rho A^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\{A^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu_{1}}A^{a}_{\nu_{2}},\rho\}\Big).

(152)

Defining
{\cal K}[\cdot]\coloneq\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}{\cal D}[\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4}\cdot\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4}]\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}
, and
K
its vectorization, we can verify that
5
5

5

Let
\delta{\cal D}\coloneq{\cal D}-\mathbb{E}_{R}{\cal D}
and
\delta{\cal K}\coloneq{\cal K}-\mathbb{E}_{R}{\cal K}
. The first equality in (
154
) follows from

\displaystyle\|\delta{\cal K}\|_{2\to 2}=\max_{X}\frac{\|\delta{\cal K}[X]\|_{2}}{\|X\|_{2}}=\max_{X}\frac{\|\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}\delta{\cal D}[\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4}X\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4}]\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}\|_{2}}{\|X\|_{2}}=\max_{\tilde{X}}\frac{\|\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}\delta{\cal D}[\tilde{X}]\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}\|_{2}}{\|\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}\tilde{X}\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4}\|_{2}}=\max_{\tilde{X}}\frac{\|\delta{\cal D}[\tilde{X}]\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2}}{\|\tilde{X}\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2}}

(153)

with
\tilde{X}\coloneq\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4}X\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4}
.
In the present work, the maximization of
X
(
\tilde{X}
) to define the induced norm is restricted to Hermitian operators.

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}-\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{D}\|_{\sigma_{\beta}^{-1},2\to 2}=\|\mathcal{K}-\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathcal{K}\|_{2\to 2}=\|K-\mathbb{E}_{R}K\|_{\infty}.

(154)

We note that, for any
\sigma_{\beta}
-DB dissipative channel
{\cal D}
, the channel
{\cal K}
is self-adjoint with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product for all bounded operators.
Furthermore
K
, the vectorization of
{\cal K}
, is given by
6
6

6

The vectorization of
{\cal D}
is

\displaystyle D=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\sum_{\mu,\nu}\eta_{\mu}\eta_{\nu}\Big((A^{a}_{\nu})^{*}\otimes A^{a}_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}I\otimes(A^{a}_{\mu})^{\dagger}A^{a}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}(A^{a}_{\nu})^{\intercal}(A^{a}_{\mu})^{*}\otimes I\Big).

(155)

Then, the vectorization of
{\cal K}
is
K=((\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4})^{\intercal}\otimes\sigma_{\beta}^{-1/4})D((\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4})^{\intercal}\otimes\sigma_{\beta}^{1/4})
.

\displaystyle K=\frac{1}{|{\bm{A}}|}\sum_{a}\sum_{\mu,\nu}\eta_{\mu}\eta_{\nu}\Big(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\beta}{4}(\mu+\nu)}(A^{a}_{\nu})^{*}\otimes A^{a}_{\mu}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{4}(\mu-\nu)}}{2}I\otimes(A^{a}_{\mu})^{\dagger}A^{a}_{\nu}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\beta}{4}(\mu-\nu)}}{2}(A^{a}_{\nu})^{\intercal}(A^{a}_{\mu})^{*}\otimes I\Big),

(156)

where
A^{\intercal}
is the transpose of
A
.

Our strategy to bound Eq. (
154
